### L3.S31 · [T] · Rose (alone)
*Purpose: the campaign book, opened — the accounting, after ten days; what the return has bought.*
> *(She opens the campaign book on the tenth evening — ten days since Bath, ten days since the Crescent — and does the accounting the way Quill taught her: in order, without emphasis, the figures doing the shouting. The warehouse: mapped, the quantities taken, the private book copied, Creech courted and firewalled. The atelier: a listening post, confirmed; Élise's scrap filed; "the purse is deep, but the purse is impatient" — the season's epigraph, in a modiste's hand. The night: ridden, the whole line, paper to press to hawker, known by heart. The hand: one hand, two errands, followed not broken. The town: the white roses in the hall, in bud. Henry: deciding. The four: ledgered, the rope holding.)*
> *(She sits back. The lamp gutters. Outside, London is doing whatever London does at this hour — the clubs filling, the* Lantern *being set in type for tomorrow, the clerk with the scarred knuckle somewhere counting gold or ordering coats or talking too much. She is not winning. She understands that, clearly, without flinching: the* Lantern *still prints, the ton still mentions, the purse is still deep. But she is no longer* losing *— and the difference between losing and not-losing, she is learning, is the whole of the war's first movement. Losing is drowning. Not-losing is swimming. She is swimming — stroke by stroke, ledger by ledger, night by night.)*
> *(She writes at the bottom of the page, in the small tight hand: "Ten days. Not winning. No longer losing." She underlines "no longer losing" once — not twice; once is enough, for now. Then she closes the book, and blows out the lamp, and sleeps the sleep of the not-losing: deep, dreamless, and — for the first time in weeks — untroubled by paper. In the morning there will be Quill, and the heavy weapon, and the law's patience. Tonight there is only the book, closed, and the dark, and the swimming.)*
> *(The house ticks around her with the particular ticking of a London house at night, so like the warehouse's ticking that she smiles in the dark. Somewhere below, Letitia is still awake; Rose can hear the faint murmur of the aunt's voice, reading aloud to herself the way she does when she cannot sleep. Poetry, probably. Something with roses in it. Augusta's door is closed. The general sleeps the sleep of the just, or does not sleep at all, but either way silently. The campaign's household, at rest. The war's first movement, complete.)*

### L3.S32 · [D] · Rose, Quill
*Purpose: the lawyer sees the arithmetic — Creech's figures become legal evidence; the heavy weapon, readied.*
> *(Quill reads the campaign book in the morning room with the particular attention of a man defusing something — page by page, the figures, the dates, the weights, the scarred knuckle, the tripling dated to the Hartwell numbers. He does not speak until he has finished. Then he closes the book — gently, the way one closes evidence — and looks at Rose over his spectacles. "Miss Hartwell," he says. "Do you know what you have* bought *me?")*
> *("Arithmetic," Rose says.)*
> *("Malice." Quill corrects her — the surgeon's precision, even now. "Proved by* money*. A libel that costs this much is not a grudge, Miss Hartwell. A grudge is cheap. This — " He taps the book. "— is an* investment*. And the law, Miss Hartwell, understands investments. The law does not understand grudges — they are too cheap to prosecute. But* money* — " He taps the book again. "— money the law understands* perfectly*.")*
> *(Rose sits very still. The morning room is quiet. Quill rises, crosses to the window, all business now — the advocate, not the adviser. "The hand first," he says. "Then the purse. Then the* face*. In that order, Miss Hartwell — the law is patient, and patience is the law's whole advantage. We follow the clerk. We do not break him — your grandmother's counsel, and sound. Hands that count gold can be made to count other things." He turns. "And when the hand leads us to the purse — " He stops. The sentence hangs unfinished, the way the important ones do. "— the purse will find that the law has been* waiting*. Patiently. The way the law waits.")*
> *("The heavy weapon," Rose says quietly. "Readied.")*
> *("Readied," Quill agrees. "Not* drawn*. Readied." He picks up the book. "There is a difference, Miss Hartwell, and it is the whole of the law. Drawn weapons frighten. Readied weapons* wait*." A small, thin smile — the closest Quill comes to warmth. "We shall wait, Miss Hartwell. Patiently. The way the law waits. And the purse — deep and impatient — will not.")*
> *(He folds his spectacles, the particular gesture of a man putting away one kind of sight and taking up another, and when he looks at her again his eyes are very clear. "Miss Hartwell," he says, "you have given me the rarest thing in law. A case with arithmetic in it.")*

> 4. [examine]Hear the order — "the hand first, then the purse, then the face." → Remembered:The order; the prosecution's sequence.

> 6. [examine]Keep "drawn weapons frighten; readied weapons wait." → Remembered:The maxim; the law's patience, defined.

### L3.S33 · [T] · Rose (alone)
*Purpose: the tempo, chosen — the war's rhythm; patience as a weapon, named.*
> *(She stands at the window after Quill has gone, and chooses the tempo — not with drama, but with the quiet click of a well-made lock. Theirs: the* Lantern's *tempo — weekly, frantic, the press thumping at midnight, the purse impatient, the clerk talking too much, the whole gutter machinery running hot. Ours: the campaign's tempo — the warehouse ticking, the atelier humming, the book filling page by page, the hand followed not broken, the law waiting patiently the way the law waits. Frantic versus patient. Hot versus ticking. The purse is impatient — Élise's sentence, the season's epigraph. Impatience makes mistakes. Patience collects them.)*
> *(She thinks of the ten days: the maps, the ledgers, the night ride, the scrap, the roses in bud. None of it was fast. All of it was* exact*. Augusta's counsel, that first morning in Bath: men with ledgers respect two things — exactness, and not wasting their time. The campaign has been exact. The campaign has not wasted its time. The* Lantern *will print on Thursday — the third number, the third Thursday — and the town will read it, and mention it, and the ton will do what the ton does. Let them. The warehouse ticks on. The atelier hums. The book fills. The hand is followed. The law waits.)*
> *(She smiles at the empty room — the small, private smile of a general who has chosen her ground. Patience is not passivity. She understands that now, clearly: passivity is waiting for things to happen. Patience is* preparing *for them — the maps, the ledgers, the listening posts, the readied weapon. The purse is deep, and the purse is impatient, and impatience — she has learned this from Élise's scrap, from the clerk's talk, from the whole hot machinery of the gutter — impatience always, always spends itself. She has only to be there when it does. With the book. With the law. With the arithmetic the gutter cannot arrange.)*
> *(She thinks of Pyke, the ink-stained proprietor, the man behind the desk she has never seen, setting tomorrow's type at midnight with the purse's impatience breathing down his neck. Hurry, the purse says. More. Faster. Double. And Pyke hurries, because hurried men make mistakes, and mistaken men print things, and printed things can be filed. She almost pities him. Then she remembers the braided ruin, and does not.)*

### L3.S34 · [D] · Rose, Lavinia
*Purpose: Lavinia's verdict — the guide's weekly report; the alliance, deepened.*
> *(Lavinia's drawing room, late afternoon — the amber light, the mirrors, the glitter at full strength. "Darling," Lavinia says, without preamble. "The week." She ticks the items off on her fingers, the rings flashing. "Creech — courted, and* won*, unless I miss my guess, which I never do. Élise — a* genius*, darling, a positive genius; the atelier hears everything and the girl writes it down, which is more than most of Whitehall manages. The night — " She looks at Rose sharply. "— ridden, unless I miss* my *guess about the boots by the door, which I do not. The clerk — one hand, two errands. The purse — deep, impatient, and* spending*." She sits back. "My verdict, darling: the best week of the war.")*
> *("The verdict being?" Rose asks — though she knows, and Lavinia knows she knows, and the asking is pure ceremony.)*
> *("That we* follow *him, darling." Lavinia leans forward, all business under the glitter. "The clerk. The scarred knuckle. Together — you and I. Not agents, not riders —* us*. I know the town's nights, darling. You know the line. Between us — " A wave of the hand. "— we shall have him* followed*, and* handled*, and* turned*, before the purse knows what has happened to its errand boy." A pause. "Its* clerk*, darling. Forgive me. The distinction is the whole of the trail.")*
> *(Rose looks at her — the glitter, the rings, the fierce competence under the performance — and understands that the alliance has just become something else: not a guide and a client, but* partners*. "Together," she says. "Tomorrow. The hand.")*
> *("Tomorrow," Lavinia agrees — and her voice has the particular brightness it gets when a plan is afoot, which is the brightest thing about her. "Supper first, darling. At* mine*. Seven o'clock. We shall plan over soup — the Crane soup, which has launched a thousand schemes. Bring the campaign book. Bring* yourself*. Leave the boots — tomorrow will be soon enough for boots.")*
> *(The rings flash as she talks, the particular flashing of a woman who uses her hands the way other women use words. "Creech's note will come, darling. The clerk will come back to Élise's. Clerks always come back; the inseam is never quite right the first time. And we — " She leans forward, and the glitter goes very still, the way it does when she means a thing. "We shall be ready, darling. That is the whole of the plan. Readiness.")*

### L3.S35 · [D] · Rose, Lavinia
*Purpose: supper at Lavinia's — the alliance, humanized; what the guide has lost to the Lantern.*
> *(The Crane supper room is small and perfect — candlelight, the famous soup, two places set with the particular intimacy of women who have stopped performing for each other. Lavinia is quieter here, the glitter banked, and Rose understands that this is the real Lavinia: not the guide, not the performance, but the woman underneath, who has been fighting this war longer than Rose has been alive. "You asked me once," Lavinia says, over the soup, "what the* Lantern *cost* me*, darling. I never answered. I shall answer now.")*
> *(Rose waits. The candles gutter. Somewhere in the house a clock ticks.)*
> *("My* brother*, darling." Lavinia says it simply — the simple way she says all the true things, which is the most glittering thing about her. "Years ago. The* Lantern's *predecessor — a dirtier sheet, if you can imagine it. It printed something about him — something* true*, darling, which is the worst kind. In the wrong order. At the wrong time. To the wrong people." She looks at her soup. "He left London. He never came back. The family — " A wave of the hand. "— the family* mentions *him, darling. The way the ton mentions everything: lightly, and forever.")*
> *("Lavinia," Rose says quietly. "I did not know.")*
> *("No one knows, darling. That is rather the point of me." Lavinia looks up, and her eyes are bright — not with glitter, but with something older. "I guide girls like you through these wars because — " She stops. "Because no one guided* me*, darling. Because my brother had no campaign book, no grandmother-general, no modiste writing things down on scraps." A pause. "Because* someone *should have followed the hand, back then. And no one did.")*
> *(Rose reaches across the table and takes Lavinia's hand — the rings, the glitter, the fierce competence, all of it. "Then we shall follow it now," she says. "Together. For him, too.")*
> *("For him, too," Lavinia agrees — and her voice has the particular steadiness it gets when she means a thing completely. "The soup plots, darling. It always has. Tomorrow — the hand.")*

### L3.S36 · [D] · Rose, Letitia
*Purpose: Letitia on Lavinia — the aunt's verdict; the alliance, permitted.*
> *("Supper at* Lavinia's*," Letitia says, over breakfast — and her voice has the particular pitch it gets when she has been thinking about something all night and has decided to be direct about it, which is alarming in an aunt. "Alone, darling. At* night*. With the* soup*." She sets down her teacup with unusual firmness. "I have been thinking, darling. About Lavinia Crane.")*
> *("Yes, Aunt?" Rose says carefully.)*
> *("She is — " Letitia searches for the word. "—* glittering*, darling. And I do not trust glittering. Glittering is how the* Lantern *dresses its lies — all sparkle and no substance." She leans forward. "But." A pause. "But she gave you Creech's name, darling. She gave you the warehouse. She gave you the* hand*. And last night — " She stops, suddenly shy. "Last night you came home* different*, darling. Quieter. As if you had — " She searches again. "— as if you had been* trusted *with something.")*
> *("I was," Rose says quietly. "Her brother, Aunt. Years ago. The* Lantern's *predecessor. Something true, in the wrong order." She looks at Letitia. "She guides girls like me because no one guided her.")*
> *(Letitia is silent for a long moment. Then she does something unexpected: she nods — once, firmly, the way Augusta nods. "Then she may keep you, darling." She picks up her teacup. "The alliance is — " She searches for the word a third time, and finds it. "—* permitted*. Provisionally. I shall be watching, darling. Aunts* watch*." A pause. "And the soup — the soup is* excellent*, I am told. If she feeds you well, I shall permit a* great *deal.")*
> *(Rose laughs — the genuine laugh, the one she has not laughed in weeks. "The soup plots, Aunt. It always has.")*
> *("Then let it plot," Letitia says firmly. "But* aim *true, darling. That is all I ask. Aim true.")*
> *(The breakfast things are still on the table: the toast rack, the marmalade, Letitia's second cup going cold the way it always does when she is being direct. Rose watches her aunt marshal the teacup like a weapon and feels something loosen in her chest. Fondness, sudden and sharp. Letitia, being direct. Augusta would call it a flanking maneuver. Rose calls it love, in the aunt's own idiom: clumsy, brave, and exactly on time.)*

### L3.S37 · [T] · Rose (alone)
*Purpose: Thursday again — the third Lantern; the doctrine, held; the household's unity.*
> *(Thursday. The third number. It arrives with the breakfast things, the way the first two did — the boy's cry in the street, the paper folded on the tray, the ton's mention already beginning upstairs and down. Rose opens it with steady hands. The accusation is there — the braided ruin, the dupes, the Hartwell numbers — dressed in the week's new malice, the purse's impatience showing at the edges like a frayed cuff. She reads it through, once, without flinching. Then she folds it, and files it in the campaign book with the other two, and goes down to breakfast.)*
> *("Well?" Augusta says — the general, at the head of the table.)*
> *("The third," Rose says. "The same. The purse is spending." She takes up her toast. "Let them print.")*
> *(Letitia breathes — the small, relieved breath of a woman who has been holding something all morning. Augusta nods — once, the general's nod. The doctrine holds: not read and raged over, but read, filed, and breakfasted past. The household is united in it now — the general, the aunt, the girl — three women at a breakfast table, unmoved by a penny paper. The* Lantern *has printed its third number. The campaign has filed its third number. The score, if anyone is keeping it, is not what the* Lantern *thinks it is.)*
> *(She thinks, going up to dress: the warehouse, mapped. The atelier, listening. The night, ridden. The hand, followed. The law, waiting. The soup, plotting. The roses, in bud. The purse is deep, and the purse is impatient, and impatience spends itself — Thursday after Thursday, number after number, gold on Creech's desk, edges up, in tens. Let them print. The campaign files. The book grows fat. The buds will open. She dresses for the day — the severe gown again, the credibility gown, the warehouse's gown — and goes down to face whatever Thursday brings, filed and breakfasted and ready.)*
> *(The* Lantern *lies on the tray, folded, already losing the day's urgency, with the particular deflation of a libel once it has been filed. By noon the ton will have mentioned it to exhaustion. By evening it will be wrapping paper. That is the thing about weekly papers: they are weekly. They come, they go, they wrap the fish. The campaign book, on the other hand, accumulates. Thursday after Thursday the file grows fat, and the fat file, one day, will be the heavy weapon.)*

> 5. [social]Receive Augusta's nod — "once, the general's nod"; the command. → Remembered:The nod; the general confirms.

### L3.S38 · [T] · Rose (alone)
*Purpose: the archive, inventoried — everything filed; tomorrow, the hand.*
> *(She opens the drawer in the evening — the campaign's archive, the drawer that has become a history — and inventories it the way Quill taught her: in order, without emphasis. Three* Lanterns*, filed in order — the first, the second, the third, the braided ruin in triplicate. Two* Gazette *cuttings — the season's verdicts, the judge's voice, Mrs. Nance Bell's paper watching it all. The Bath maps — the supply, the distribution, the whole geography of the gutter. Élise's scrap — "the purse is deep, but the purse is impatient," in the modiste's careful hand. The white rose — pressed, from Letitia's posy, the clean name in bud, preserved.)*
> *(She touches each thing in turn — the paper, the cuttings, the maps, the scrap, the rose — and understands that the drawer has become something she did not plan. Not a file anyone organized. A record the war kept of itself, in her hand, in a drawer in a London house. Someday — not soon, but someday — someone will open this drawer and understand the whole of it: the ruin, the return, the warehouse, the night, the hand. The archive does not know it is an archive. It thinks it is a drawer. That is the best kind.)*
> *(She closes the drawer — gently, the way one closes a book one intends to reopen. Tomorrow: the hand. Lavinia at seven — no, Lavinia* tomorrow*; the plan made over soup, the pursuit beginning. The clerk with the scarred knuckle, somewhere in London tonight, counting gold or ordering coats or talking too much — unaware that two women have decided to follow him, handle him, turn him. She blows out the lamp. The drawer sits in the dark, full of paper. The paper waits. The hand waits. Tomorrow, the following begins.)*
> *(Last of all she takes out the white rose, pressed flat between the pages, the petals thin as paper, the bud's promise preserved in two dimensions. It crackles faintly when she touches it. Letitia's posy. The hall vase. The ton asking what it means. All of it here, in this flat flower. "The clean name," she whispers to the empty room. "In bud." She puts it back carefully, between the scrap and the cuttings, and closes the drawer on the whole of it: the war, in a drawer, waiting for morning.)*

### L3.S39 · Gazette sting
*Purpose: the Gazette sting — the paper war's public face; the ton, baffled; Bell, watching.*
> *(The* Gazette *arrives with the evening post — Mrs. Nance Bell's paper, the judge's voice, the season's verdict rendered in print. Rose opens it with the particular attention one gives a letter from a strict but fair-minded friend. And there it is — the sting, the season's third, the paragraph the whole ton will be quoting by tomorrow: "Miss Hartwell studies paper. The ton, which does not read it, is baffled." She reads it twice. Then she laughs — the genuine laugh, the one she has not laughed in weeks.)*
> *(Augusta reads it over her shoulder — the general, at her most still. "Bell," she says. "Watching." A pause. "The* ton *does not read paper, child. It reads* people*. And it has just been told — by the only paper that matters — that you are* studying*. Not scheming. Not raging.* Studying*." She sits back. "Do you understand what that is? It is* permission*, child. The Gazette has just given the ton permission to be baffled — and bafflement, in the ton, is the beginning of* interest*.")*
> *(Letitia reads it third — the aunt, at her most bright. "Baffled," she says, delighted. "The ton is* baffled*, darling. By* you*. Studying* paper*." She claps her hands. "Oh, it is* perfect*. The* Lantern *prints lies about you, and the* Gazette *prints that you* study *— and the ton, which understands neither printing nor studying, is* baffled *by the* studying*." She looks at Rose. "Darling — you are* winning*. Not the war — the* bafflement*. And the bafflement is the beginning.")*
> *(Rose folds the* Gazette *— gently, the way one folds a verdict — and files it with the cuttings in the drawer. "Miss Hartwell studies paper." The ton is baffled. Bell is watching. The paper war has a public face now — not the* Lantern's *shriek, but the* Gazette's *measured sentence. And the measured sentence, she is learning, is the one that lasts.)*
> *(By noon the paragraph is everywhere: the clubs, the drawing rooms, the milliners, quoted with the particular relish the ton reserves for sentences that sting someone else. "Miss Hartwell studies paper." The ladies repeat it over their embroidery. The gentlemen repeat it over their port. And somewhere, in an office over a printing house, Mrs. Nance Bell, proprietor, judge, the only editor in London who signs her own name, sets tomorrow's type with ink on her fingers and watches her sentence do its work. The* Gazette *does not shriek. It measures. And the measured sentence is the one that lasts.)*

### L3.S40 · cliffhanger · Rose (alone)
*Purpose: the purse quickens — Creech's note; the payment increased; the blank center, trembling.*
> *(The note comes by hand in the morning — Creech's hand, the merchant's careful script, on warehouse paper. Rose opens it at breakfast, and the toast stops halfway to her mouth. "Miss Hartwell — " the note reads. "The clerk came again yesterday. The payment is* increased *— by half, miss. Half again as much gold, for half again as much paper. The purse is* impatient*, miss — I thought of your grandmother's phrase, though I do not know where she got it. The reams are to* double*, miss. Double. I thought you should know.")*
> *(Augusta reads it over her shoulder — the general, at her most still. Letitia stops breathing — the aunt, at her most pale. Rose reads it a third time. Half again as much gold. Double the reams. The purse — deep, impatient — has decided to* spend *its way out of the problem: more paper, more ink, more boys, more extra-pay notes. The* Lantern*, doubled. The ruin, doubled. The investment, doubled — the investors expecting their return, the return being her, priced to the ream, counted to the sovereign.)*
> *(She folds the note — gently, the way one folds a warning — and puts it in the campaign book, with the figures. The blank center of her map is trembling now — the purse, quickened by impatience, spending faster, printing more, the whole hot machinery running hotter. Élise was right. The purse is deep. The purse is impatient. And impatience — she has learned this, Thursday after Thursday — impatience spends itself. The question is no longer whether the purse will spend itself out. The question is what it will* break *on the way down — and whether she will be standing clear when it does. She looks at Augusta. Augusta looks at her. The toast, forgotten, goes cold.)*
> *(The breakfast room goes very quiet, with the particular quiet of a house receiving bad news with good manners. Letitia's hand finds Rose's under the table and holds on. Augusta folds the note once, precisely, and puts it in her pocket, the general securing intelligence. "Double," Augusta says at last. "The purse doubles its bet." She looks at Rose, and her eyes are very clear. "Then we double our watching, child. Our waiting. Our arithmetic. The purse is impatient. We are not." A pause. "That is the whole of the advantage. If it is enough. We shall see if it is enough.")*

> 2. [examine]Read "increased by half — the reams to double." → Remembered:The increase; the investment doubles.
> 3. [social]Watch Augusta still — Letitia pale; the household. → Remembered:The household's stillness; the news lands.
> 4. [examine]Understand "spend its way out" — the purse's logic. → Remembered:The logic; impatience spends.
